The Wild proper is none of these things. The trees are enormous. The undergrowth is dense in a way that a wild land without a gardener should be. The path — there is no path, there is only the direction of travel — requires constant reading of terrain. 

The party will spend roughly three weeks traveling from the Aven to Queen Elandra's court, assuming the goblinoid pursuit is resolved in the first week and they can move at normal travel pace thereafter. Their journey will not be uneventful. 

**Travel Pace and Conditions**

Normal overland travel rules apply, with the following modifications:
- Travel pace is reduced to three-quarters normal in dense forest without a trail (difficult terrain for half of each day's travel, on average).
- Foraging is productive; the Wild is old-growth and rich. A character with Survival can comfortably supplement rations for the entire party with a DC 12 check.
- Navigation requires a Survival check every three days; a failure does not result in getting lost outright, but costs one day of travel and may indicate the terrain is directing them rather than being neutral.

**First Encounters with the Fey**

The party will not reach Elandra's court without being noticed first. The Wild's smaller fey inhabitants — sprites, brownies, tiny creatures without names the party would know — will have been watching since shortly after the Aven crossing. They do not approach openly; they observe and occasionally intervene in small ways: a path that is subtly easier than it should be, food that has been left at the edge of a clearing, a branch that catches a party member's arm just before they would have stepped onto unstable ground.

The first direct fey encounter, roughly a week into the Wild, is a single dryad who steps out of an oak ahead of the party and waits. She is not hostile. She is not particularly warm. She will answer direct questions about the terrain and the journey ahead with reasonable accuracy, and she will ask the party one question in return, which she will pose plainly and expect answered plainly:

*"You are from the country of the newcomers. Do you know what was here before it?"*

What she means — and what she will confirm if pressed, or elaborate if the party engages seriously — is not a riddle. She means the lands that were the Wild before Haven's borders were drawn. She means the goblinoid settlements that were in those lands, the farms and the burial grounds and the trade routes. She was there. Her tree was there. Some of her sisters died when those settlements were cleared. She is not asking because she expects the party to have an answer. She is asking because the question matters, and because nobody from Haven has ever asked her what was here before.

*This is the first opportunity for Revelation 2 — The Displacement. A party that engages seriously with the dryad and asks about Haven's history from the fey perspective will receive a partial revelation: confirmation that the goblinoids were displaced, and that the fey witnessed it. The full confirmation comes in Aldenmere.*

A second brief encounter, perhaps three days later, involves a group of four or five pixies who take a mischievous interest in the party — not hostile, but testing. They will play tricks (small items misplaced, one character's boots tied together while sleeping) and watch the reaction. A party that responds with humor or with genuine good nature will find the pixies becoming briefly useful, pointing toward water sources and warning of a patch of quicksand half a day ahead. A party that responds with aggression or cold dismissal will find the tricks escalating until they stop being funny — nothing dangerous, but irritating and time-consuming. Fey etiquette applies in both cases: the party earns what their conduct deserves.

*These encounters are brief and do not have stat blocks or detailed mechanics. They are atmospheric and revelatory. The GM should develop them according to table interest, spending as much or as little time as the party's engagement warrants.*

**Random Encounters: Through the Wild**

Check once per day of travel; roll 1d20, encounter on a 15 or higher. If the goblinoid pursuit is still active, substitute a goblinoid band on any result of 12 or lower instead.

| d12 | Encounter | CR | Notes |
|-----|-----------|-----|-------|
| 1 | Wyvern | 7 | Hunting; swoops at tree level, retreats to height to assess before committing |
| 2 | Redcaps (1d3+1) | 6 ea. | Fey; test the party before attacking; correct fey courtesy earns grudging respect and rough directions |
| 3 | Hill giant | 7 | Eating; demands tribute in food; can be talked down, bribed, or tricked |
| 4 | Shambling mound | 6 | Concealed beneath a boggy stream crossing; the only warning is the smell |
| 5 | Greater barghest | 7 | Has been pacing the party for hours at the edge of visibility before revealing itself; prefers prey that runs |
| 6 | Werebear (in bear form) | 7 | Traveler who has lived in the Wild for years; not hostile; in human form will share route knowledge including Elandra's grove location and Wild Hunt patterns near the equinox |
| 7 | Nymph | 7 | At a pool brighter than it should be; dangerous if approached without fey courtesy; will answer questions about terrain, local fey, and Haven's history from the outside |
| 8 | Manticore with two young | 5+3+3 | Stalks the party a full day before attacking in concert; threatening the young commits the parents immediately |
| 9 | Dire wolf pack (5) | 3 ea. | Territorial alpha; Handle Animal or Wild Empathy DC 20 may divert; alpha defeated in round one causes a morale check |
| 10 | Trolls (2) | 5 ea. | Mid-argument over a fallen log; party can slip past with a group Stealth DC 14 while they're distracted, or resolve their dispute for free passage |
| 11 | Korred (4) | 4 ea. | Fey; block the path with a stone barrier; demand a physical challenge (Str DC 22, Acrobatics DC 20, or Con DC 18 endurance test); cheating earns respect; failure costs something small and personal |
| 12 | Green hag | 5 | Disguised as an old woman tending a fire; her information is entirely accurate and costs something personal, never valuable; attacking her without cause makes the Wild less navigable for days |

---

### Queen Elandra's Court

The party does not find Elandra's court, the court finds them.

On a morning roughly three weeks after the Aven crossing, the party wakes to find that the forest around their camp is different. The trees are older. The light is different — not the quality of time of day, but something about the frequency of it, as if it has traveled further to arrive. Within an hour of travel, the path — which was not a path — has become a path. The GM should allow Survival checks to notice that the direction of easy travel is converging toward something, and that the animals, which have been normal animals for the past two weeks, are absent.

The path ends at the court.

> *The path narrows. The trees grow closer together than trees have any right to, their canopies interlocking overhead until there is no sky left. The light filtering through is old light, green-gold, the color of sunlight that has been passing through leaves for centuries. Then the trees give way — abruptly, without transition — and you are standing at the edge of a clearing the size of a great hall, its floor soft grass instead of stone, its columns, those of stately birch trees perfectly placed in neat rows, its ceiling an unbroken vault of oak leaves so densely woven that no rain could penetrate them and the sound of the wind cannot reach below.*
>
> *There are no walls, and yet there are walls. Painted screens of figured silk stand between the trees at intervals, their illustrations moving slowly in a wind that is not present. Furniture of impossible intricacy sits arranged in clusters — chairs shaped from a single piece of pale wood, tables balanced on one carved leg, cabinets whose doors hang open on hinges of braided vine. Creatures you do not have names for stand or sit or drift between these arrangements: some tall and luminous, some no larger than a child's fist with wings like damselflies, some that are neither here nor not-here and are easier to see when you do not look at them directly. The sound of music comes from somewhere you cannot locate, and the music has no instrument you can name.*
>
> *In the center of the court, flanked by two birch trees that have grown into the shape of pillars, stands a throne carved from the heartwood of an oak that is still alive — you can see the bark, the grain that continues from the living wood into the carved seat without interruption, the roots that reach down through the grass into the earth below. The woman seated on it has eyes the color of shallow water over pale stone, hair that has never been the same color twice since you started looking at it, and an expression of complete and relaxed attention. She has been expecting you for some time. You understand this immediately, though you could not say how.*

**Queen Elandra**

Elandra is the sovereign of a territory that does not have borders in any sense Haven's people would recognize; it is defined by her, and it extends as far as her attention and her authority reach, which varies. She is ancient in the way that very old fey are ancient — not obviously aged, but carrying a quality of accumulated time that is felt rather than seen, a sense that she has watched things before and knows how they tend to go. She is not patient in any warm or generous sense of the word. She is patient the way deep water is patient.

She is also, genuinely, mercurial. Her moods shift with a naturalness that a human courtier would spend years trying to fake — she finds something funny, and she is briefly and completely delighted, and then she is not, and neither state has any obvious cause in the outside world. This is her nature, not the vapid performance of a bored noble. The GM should play her as a queen who has never once needed to perform anything; her court adapts to her, not the reverse.

She knows the party is from Haven. She knows, in a general way, why they are traveling east. She will receive them formally, with the ritual courtesy of a fey court, and she will state her position without ambiguity:

*"You are traveling through my territory without my permission. I did not grant it because I was not asked. You may remain as my guests, or you may leave immediately. If you leave, you will find that the path east remains closed to you until I say otherwise. If you remain, we will reach an agreement about the terms of your passage."*

The agreement has two possible forms.

**Fey Etiquette at Court**

All rules of fey etiquette from the Introduction apply for the entirety of the party's time at court. Elandra is not a formal enforcer — she does not keep score and announce violations — but offenses against fey courtesy will register in her expression, in the temperature of the room (metaphorically and sometimes literally), and eventually in her willingness to negotiate. A party that conducts itself with genuine respect — not performance, but the actual substance of treating their host as someone whose authority and traditions merit consideration — will find Elandra responsive and occasionally forthcoming. A party that treats fey etiquette as a game to be won will find the rules becoming more stringent.

*Time moves differently within the court. A stay that feels like two or three days takes one day of material-world time. The party should be aware of this only after the fact, when they exit the court and note the position of the sun.*

**Bargaining for Passage**

At this point, the party may attempt to bargain for passage, or ask the queen her price. 

A party that conducts itself with perfect fey courtesy throughout the encounter — no violations of etiquette, appropriate gifts if offered, respect for the court's hierarchy and customs — may attempt to negotiate their passage through careful bargaining. This requires a Diplomacy check (DC 28) and takes the form of a negotiated exchange of value. The party must identify something it can offer that Elandra does not have and would value; a party that has been paying attention will have several options (information about Haven's current political state, skills or abilities of genuine use to the court, a sincere promise of a future favor from a party of named royal agents). Elandra negotiates seriously and does not play games at the table; she finds games more satisfying before the bargaining begins.

A failed Diplomacy check does not foreclose the challenges or services — it simply means the direct route is closed. 

**The Three Challenges**

If the party wishes to impress Elandra sufficiently to earn free passage — a more direct route to departure, and potentially to the magic items she gives willingly to those who genuinely delight her — they may take up any of the three challenges. Completing all three earns the maximum possible regard. Completing one, particularly brilliantly, earns nearly as much. Failing all three, after attempting them, loses no ground if the attempts were made in good faith and with genuine effort; Elandra respects the attempt. Refusing to attempt any of them and requesting the services instead is also acceptable.

*Challenge One — The Song of Beauty*

One party member must perform a song — or any other musical performance, broadly interpreted — of extraordinary beauty before the court. This is not a technical challenge; it is an artistic one.

The Perform check required is DC 26. A character with the bardic performance ability, the appropriate Perform skill, and the Charisma to match may make this with reasonable reliability; a character without these tools is unlikely to succeed without assistance from a party member or an item. The Perform check alone is not sufficient: the character must also be able to describe the performance — what are they playing or singing, how are they doing it, and why is it designed to delight or move the fey specifically? A technically perfect performance in service of a poor artistic concept fails regardless of the die result. A creative and well-considered concept can reduce the DC by up to 4, at the GM's discretion.

The court's response to a successful performance is unambiguous: the assembled fey will fall silent — all of them, simultaneously — and remain silent for a moment after the final note. This is the highest compliment they can give. Elandra will be still in a way she has not been still since the party arrived.

*Challenge Two — The Contest of Riddles*

Jackanapes is the queen's fool, a position he has held for approximately two centuries. He is elderly, short even for a gnome, and possessed of a quality of focused attention that makes him seem larger than he is. He has a very long memory for riddles. He has heard most of them.

The contest is structured: the first to correctly answer three of their opponent's riddles wins. A riddle that the audience does not find genuinely clever — too easy, too obvious, or poorly constructed — does not count as a scored riddle; Jackanapes or the party member must pose another. The audience serves as a fair and enthusiastic judge on this point; fey love a riddle contest and will groan openly at a bad riddle regardless of who poses it.

Cheating is actively encouraged by the rules of the contest. Neither party is obligated to disclose this; it is simply understood. A party member who obtains the answer from a knowing audience member through a whispered exchange or subtle gesture, and who manages to do so without Jackanapes catching them at it, will receive the respect due to a clever cheat. Jackanapes may attempt to cheat as well. If caught, he will be delighted.

Sample riddles Jackanapes is known to use:

- *"I have cities, but no houses live in them. I have mountains, but no trees grow there. I have water, but no fish swim in it. I have roads, but no carts travel them. What am I?"* (A map.)
- *"The more you take of me, the more you leave behind. What am I?"* (Footsteps.)
- *"I am not alive, but I grow. I have no lungs, but I need air. I have no mouth, but water kills me. What am I?"* (Fire.)
- *"I speak without a mouth and hear without ears. I have no body, but I come alive with wind. What am I?"* (An echo.)

A party member who wins the riddle contest does so on the merit of the players' own engagement with the format; the GM should not simply allow the skill check to substitute for an actual riddle exchange. A Bluff check (DC 16) can model a successful cheat attempt; a Sense Motive check (DC 16) from Jackanapes's side determines whether he catches it.

*Challenge Three — The Magical Duel*

Princess Mara is Elandra's daughter. Her father was a faerie — a creature of pure First World substance, with no mortal ancestry on that side — and this shows: she is extraordinarily short, even for a half-elf of her approximate age, with a density of freckles across her face and forearms that is striking rather than merely notable, and a manner of dress that suggests she learned to clothe herself from animals and found the results acceptable. She is also a powerful sorceress, in the same bloodline as her mother, and she has been at court long enough to know every trick in it.

The magical duel is not fought to injure. The goal is to embarrass the opponent — to catch them in a failed spell, to counter them with something unexpected, to create a moment the audience will remember. Points are awarded by audience acclaim: if the assembled court laughs, gasps appreciatively, or falls silent at a particular moment, that is a point to the party performing it. The first duelist to earn three points of audience approval wins.

Counterspells are allowed and scored. Successfully countering one of Mara's spells earns a point if done with visible flair; simply negating the effect earns no point, because competence is not inherently entertaining. Cheating is permitted and earns points if it is sufficiently audacious.

Mara fights as a 9th-level sorcerer, but with strict limits: she is not trying to harm her opponent and her spells are selected for theatrical effect. She has a strong preference for illusions and enchantments, and she finds straightforward blasting unsporting. If the party member dueling her attempts straightforward blasting rather than theatrical magic, she will adapt and become harder to impress. If they fight cleverly, she will push back harder and enjoy it more; she is the sort of person who is more dangerous when having fun.

A party member who succeeds in impressing Mara — particularly one who fights with creativity and good humor — will find that she becomes, briefly and genuinely, interested in them. She is not warm in the way her mother is not warm, but she is curious, which is its own form of attention.

**The Three Services**

If the party declines the challenges or fails them, Elandra will offer an alternative: three services that constitute adequate payment for permission to cross her territory.

*Service One — The Bracelet:* Elandra has a bracelet — silver, set with river-polished green stones, made by a dwarven craftsman in Kharas roughly forty years ago — that was taken from her personal collection by a giant named Grolt when he was a peddler and she made the mistake of accepting his hospitality. Grolt has since gone mad (the details are unclear and Elandra does not appear interested in them) and lives in the hills two days northeast of the court, where he has accumulated a hoard composed of items he has taken from travelers and things he has convinced himself are valuable. The bracelet is in there somewhere. The party should bring it back.

*Grolt is a mad hill giant (use standard hill giant statistics with the addition of the confused condition, which the GM can treat as making him unpredictable in combat rather than reliably hostile — he may attack, he may ask the party if they have seen his mother, he may attempt to trade something from his hoard for something in the party's possession). The bracelet is at the bottom of the hoard's second layer; finding it without searching the entire hoard requires a DC 18 Perception check.*

*Service Two — The Satyr Groom:* There is a tribe of satyrs living in the oak forest to the south of the court who have been at odds with Elandra for three hundred years over a misunderstanding that she will not elaborate on. (The satyrs' version of events, which the party can obtain by going to the tribe, is that Elandra attended one of their festivals uninvited, consumed an unspecified quantity of wine, made certain promises, and then denied all memory of the promises when they were brought to her attention. Elandra's version is that the satyrs have a very flexible relationship with the concept of a promise. Both versions are plausible.) Her daughter Mara is of an age to be formally courted. Elandra wants a suitable groom from the satyr tribe, which will require the party to act as intermediary between two parties who have not spoken civilly in three centuries. The satyrs will be amenable if approached with good humor, flattery, and an understanding of their customs. They will not be approached civilly by anyone who arrives citing Elandra's authority as the reason for the request.*

*This is a roleplaying encounter, not a combat encounter. The satyrs are not hostile unless treated with condescension. A successful Diplomacy check (DC 18) with appropriate social engagement — showing genuine respect for their customs, hearing their side of the history, and framing the request as an opportunity rather than an obligation — will result in an agreement to send a delegation to the court.*

*Service Three — The Hippogriff:* A wild hippogriff has taken territory in the meadows at the forest's edge, roughly half a day's travel from the court. It has injured two of Elandra's messengers and is currently ungovernable by any fey approach to the matter (fey methods of animal-taming are persuasive rather than authoritative, and this hippogriff is not in the mood to be persuaded). The party must tame it — or at least make it tractable enough to be led to the court — by means acceptable to a fey queen, meaning no harmful magic and no breaking of the animal's spirit. Mechanical taming (Handle Animal checks with appropriate time investment, DC 22 for the first successful check, DC 18 thereafter) is acceptable. A party with a character who can communicate with animals or has the Wild Empathy ability has a significant advantage.*

**Gifts and Information**

A party that has impressed Elandra — through challenges, through services, through bargaining, or simply through conduct that she found remarkable — receives gifts: one magic item per party member, drawn from the court's stores. These items are fey in character: items of concealment, grace, speed, and attunement to the natural world. Weapons are not typically among them, though the GM may make exceptions for an exceptional performance in the duel.

*Sample items appropriate for a level 7 party: Cloak of Elvenkind, Boots of Elvenkind, Amulet of Natural Armor +2, a Ring of Feather Falling, a Wand of Cure Moderate Wounds (10 charges). The specific items should be chosen based on party composition and what will actually be useful. Elandra pays attention; she does not give gifts that are merely decorative.*

Elandra will share information if asked with genuine respect and a real question — not a transactional "what do you know about X" but a question that treats the answer as valuable. She knows the following and will share each if the appropriate question is asked:

- *The Wild Hunt:* "There is a Hunt active in this territory. It rides on the autumnal equinox — not in three weeks, not in two months, but this year, this equinox. You are traveling east at a pace that may reach the mountain passes around that time. I would think carefully about this."
- *The mountains:* "The passes are navigable until early winter. Past that, they are not. The timing matters."
- *The lands east of the mountains:* "There are people there. They are not Haven's people. They know things about your kingdom that your kingdom does not know about itself. This is not a warning, it is information."
- *The displacement:* If the party asks directly about the relationship between Haven's founding and the goblinoids, or about what the Wild was before Haven's people arrived, Elandra will answer plainly. She was there. She remembers the smoke from the settlements being cleared in the eastern earldoms. She remembers the goblinoids retreating. She does not editorialize; she describes what she saw. *This is the partial Revelation 2. The full confirmation comes in Aldenmere, but a party that asks Elandra the right question will not be able to unsee her answer.*

**Level 7 Gate:** Awarded when the party departs Elandra's court by any means.

---

### Approach to the Mountains

East of Elandra's court, the Wild changes again. The forest thins — not to clearings, but to older growth, the trees wider-spaced and taller, the undergrowth almost absent as if the canopy above has been filtering light for so long that nothing survives at ground level that cannot grow in near-darkness. The terrain begins to rise. The mountains are visible before the party reaches them: a grey wall at the edge of the world, imprecise in distance, present in a way that makes the eye uncertain how far away they actually are.

Goblinoid patrols are less frequent in this part of the Wild — this is the edge of the territory, and the raiding bands do not maintain a presence this far from their strongholds. Fey encounters remain possible. The party is roughly ten days from the first mountain approaches, and perhaps two weeks from the passes proper.

The weather has been summer. It will not remain summer.

**Random Encounters: Approach to the Mountains**

Check once per day of travel; roll 1d20, encounter on a 14 or higher. Terrain is more open and creatures are more aggressive near the passes. As the autumnal equinox draws within five days, add 1 to the encounter roll threshold (encounter on 13 or higher) and replace any result of 10 on the table with the Ghost of the Taken regardless of the normal die result.

| d12 | Encounter | CR | Notes |
|-----|-----------|-----|-------|
| 1 | Chimera | 8 | Regular patrol route through this section; attacks without posturing |
| 2 | Stone giant | 8 | Surveying the approaches from a high ridge; not hunting; will answer questions briefly and factually if the party shows peaceful intent |
| 3 | Wyverns, bonded pair | 7 ea. | Defending nest approaches; pursue for one hour before returning to the nest |
| 4 | Bulette | 7 | No warning except ground vibration two rounds before emergence |
| 5 | Harpy choir (4) | 4 ea. | Captivating song (Will DC 17); lures party off-path into a steep-sided gully; terrain advantage is significant |
| 6 | Leucrottas (2) | 5 ea. | Mimicking a child crying and a wounded horse; sounds come from a narrow defile that appears to be a shortcut; Sense Motive DC 18 to doubt |
| 7 | Centaur war party (6) | 3 ea. | Following since the previous day; approach openly; want to know who is crossing their territory; honest answers earn rough mountain guidance; evasiveness or condescension adds two days to the journey |
| 8 | Annis hag | 6 | Disguised as an injured dwarven traveler; tests willingness to help the defenseless; Perception DC 20 or Sense Motive DC 16 to see through it; attacks from ambush once the party is in poor position |
| 9 | Peryton flock (4) | 4 ea. | Migrating; hungry; confident formation, obvious magic, or large numbers may deter without combat — they assess for two rounds before committing |
| 10 | Ghost of the taken | 7 | Spirit of a traveler seized by last year's Wild Hunt, unable to pass on; marked by personal effects on the path (sword in the earth, cloak on a branch); not hostile; can describe the Wild Hunt in vivid, personal terms including the sound of the horns and what happens when you hear the hounds but cannot see them |
| 11 | Griffon pair | 4 ea. | Nesting in the rock outcroppings ahead; aggressive to large groups passing below; deterred by an offering of roughly 20 lbs. of fresh game (Survival check to obtain from local terrain) |
| 12 | Phantom stag | — | A white stag appears, regards the party, then walks north toward the mountain passes; does not respond to magic; following it leads to a sheltered campsite — unused for decades, stocked with serviceable firewood and a clean spring |

*Chapter 3 begins as the party reaches the mountain approaches and the autumnal equinox draws near.*

---

## Chapter 3: The Mountain Pass
*(Levels 7–8)*

### Chapter Overview

Chapter 3 is the fulcrum of Act II. It opens with a confrontation the party cannot avoid, continues through a winter they did not plan for, and closes with the first sight of what lies beyond Haven's reach. The Wild Hunt cannot be hidden from. The winter cannot be hurried. Both demand something of the party that the road east has not asked yet: patience, judgment under pressure, and the ability to form something like relationship with people who are not Haven's people.

**Chapter 3 Milestone Leveling:**
- *Level 8* — awarded upon resolution of the Wild Hunt encounter (escape from the Hunt's grip by any means, or survival of the three waves and the confrontation with Tellaris)

---

### The Autumnal Equinox

The party enters the mountain approaches in early autumn. The weather has been good. The passes are still open. The journey is not going quickly — the terrain demands it — but there is no immediate cause for alarm, and the mountains themselves are extraordinary to look at: the grey wall has resolved into individual peaks, some of them snow-capped even now, with cloud formations that move at different speeds and in different directions on different levels of the sky.

The equinox falls on the journey's third week in the mountain foothills. The party may or may not know the date; a character with Knowledge (nature) or Survival can track the seasons, and the Ghost of the Taken encounter from Chapter 2's encounter table will have warned them if it occurred. The equinox itself falls on a clear evening — unseasonably warm, the light golden and then amber and then the specific dark red of a sky that has too much atmosphere between the eye and the sun. The animals are wrong. Not absent, as they were before Elandra's court, but wrong: moving in the wrong directions, moving too fast, and then suddenly not moving at all.

> *You have made camp where the path widens and the ground is level — practical decisions, the kind you have been making for weeks. The fire is good. The night is clear and cold in the way that autumn nights in high country are cold: a dry cold, with stars very bright and very still.*
>
> *Then the sound begins.*
>
> *It is horns first. More horns than you can count, at different pitches and from different distances, but all of them moving. Then hooves — not the rhythm of a controlled pace but the thundering, irregular beat of a full gallop, and more of it than should be possible from any number of horses you could picture. Then something else: voices, if voices can be described as the sound of need made audible, the kind of sound that makes the body want to move before the mind understands why.*
>
> *The light appears through the trees to the north. It is not fire. It is the pale, cold, moving light of a procession — and then the trees thin, and you can see it: a river of light and motion and terrible beauty coming directly toward you, and it is not slowing down.*

---

### The Wild Hunt

The Wild Hunt is not an encounter in the usual sense. It is a force, like a flood or a fire, and it treats the distinction between joining and being consumed as largely semantic.

The Hunt is a procession of the First World's most predatory sovereign tradition: fey nobles on black horses, their antler-crowned heads high, their eyes reflecting light that is not present; mortal men and women caught up in the Hunt's perimeter, running without knowing why, faces empty of the particular quality of personhood; animals — wolves, stags, great cats, birds that should not fly at night — moving in the same direction with the same purposeful haste; and stranger things at the procession's edges that are easier to describe as shapes than as creatures.

The Wild Hunt cannot be hidden from. Stealth checks, invisibility, and magical concealment fail against the Hunt's awareness, which is territorial and instinctive rather than perceptual. The Hunt does not search; it sweeps. Being in its path is being found.

**The Lure**

As the Hunt approaches, every party member must succeed at a Will save (DC 22) or be drawn into it — not compelled by force but by something that feels like invitation, the way a rip current feels like an invitation to stop fighting the water. A character who fails the save begins moving toward the Hunt, their face taking on the same absent quality as the other enthralled mortals in the procession. They are not harmed and they will not be harmed; they are simply moving.

A character who succeeds at the Will save retains full awareness and volition. They can use a standard action to assist an enthralled adjacent ally, granting that ally an immediate additional Will save at the same DC (+2 bonus from assistance). This is the only way to bring enthralled characters back before the procession has passed.

**Path A: The First World**

If all party members fail their saves, or if the party voluntarily steps into the Hunt's path, they are swept up and the Hunt carries them. The experience is not unpleasant in a conventional way — it is overwhelming rather than painful, the way standing in a very loud river is overwhelming — and it ends when the Hunt passes through a gap in the world's fabric that is not visible until you are through it.

On the far side of that gap is the First World.

*The First World* is the oldest rough draft of everything the gods eventually refined into the material plane. It is not inferior to the material world — it is rawer, more immediate, more itself. The trees are the idea of trees: impossibly tall, impossibly colorful, the bark detailed in a way that invites examination and then yields more detail on examination than any real bark should contain. The sky contains three different suns of different sizes and colors, which move independently. The ground underfoot is soft and alive in a way that is not unpleasant but is constant, as if the earth has opinions. Time does not pass in the First World the way it passes at home. The party will not be there long by any subjective measure. When they return, a year and a day will have passed in the material world.

The Hunt deposits them without ceremony. The last sound before the procession is gone is a burst of laughter from the riders at the front — the Lords of the Hunt, who have learned, in the way the Hunt always eventually learns things about those it sweeps up, that these are royal agents of Haven's Crown on a sacred quest to recover a founding artifact. This is, to the Lords, extremely funny. Most of the Hunt's accidental captives simply keep running and never find their way back. The party is not most captives, but the Lords are not going to tell them that.

*Finding the way back* requires either a Survival check (DC 25, extended action requiring 1d6 weeks of searching for a thinning — a place where the First World's membrane meets the material world closely enough to pass through) or establishing contact with a First World inhabitant who is willing to guide them. The First World's inhabitants are a GM discretionary encounter; they are fey of various dispositions, protean entities that regard the party with something between curiosity and confusion, and creatures that do not have names in any mortal language. Not all of them are hostile. None of them are indifferent. A party that engages with them on their own terms rather than Haven's terms may find the experience of the First World transformative rather than merely punishing; what they learn there, about the nature of the fey and about the original versions of things Haven calls its own, should be treated as a bonus partial revelation for Revelation 2.

*Returning from the First World:* The party emerges a year and a day after they entered. In terms of the campaign, this means the following:
- The raid Maranth was preparing has already happened, whether well or poorly coordinated depending on Act I outcomes
- Haven has had a year to develop crises without the party's presence — the GM should note one or two Act I consequences that have progressed to their next stage
- The mountain passes have reopened (it is the following autumn), and the party has lost no physical possessions but must re-assess their supplies
- Harold II knows the party did not return on any expected schedule; he does not know why; this is not cause for despair but for concern
- The party levels to 8 upon returning

**Path B: The Resistance**

Characters who retain their will can fight free of the Hunt. This requires defeating what the Hunt sends against them: three waves of escalating difficulty, followed by a direct challenge from one of the Hunt's lords.

There is no escape between waves. If the party attempts to run, the Hunt is faster, and the enthralled mortals at the procession's edge will close the gaps. The party can take short rests between waves if they act immediately after a wave ends; the Hunt gives them ten minutes between waves, because the Hunt is not in a hurry.

**Wave One**

*Three Redcaps (CR 6 each).* The Hunt's vanguard and its dark entertainment: small, iron-shod, cackling, their hats the deep red of wounds left open. They are faster than they look and they fight dirty — targeting isolated characters, attacking sleeping or prone targets, coordinating without explicit communication. They treat the fight as sport but they will kill without hesitation.

*Between waves:* A ten-minute window. The sounds of the Hunt — horns, hooves, voices — continue in the distance.

**Wave Two**

*Two Wild Hunt Hounds (CR 8 each; see Pathfinder Bestiary 4).* These are not dogs in any sense the party will recognize from Haven's kennels. They are the size of ponies, black as ink, with silver eyes and voices that are not barks but something closer to laughter in a register that should not exist. Their attacks carry a curse (Will DC 18 or –4 to AC for 24 hours, cumulative); their natural weapons are treated as cold iron and magic for purposes of overcoming DR. They do not tire and they do not feel pain in any way the party will be able to use as leverage.

*Between waves:* Another ten-minute window. The Hunt has noticed the party is fighting rather than running. The quality of attention in the darkness has changed.

**Wave Three**

*One Wild Hunt Scout (CR 9; see Pathfinder Bestiary 4) and two shadow demons (CR 7 each).* The Scout is the advance force of the Hunt's main body — a tall, antlered rider on a horse that is not entirely present, whose weapons are cold iron and whose spellcasting draws on the Hunt's own authority. The shadow demons are not permanent members of the Hunt; they are attracted to the spectacle of mortals fighting the impossible. They are indifferent to whose side they assist, but the Scout has spoken to them, briefly, in a language the party cannot follow, and whatever was said has aligned their interests for now.

*After this wave:* Silence. Then, from the darkness at the edge of the procession's light: a single rider, separated from the rest.

---

### Tellaris

The rider who approaches after the third wave has no urgency. He brings his horse to a walk before it is visible, and it walks the last distance with the ease of something that has arrived somewhere it was going to arrive anyway. He is tall for a half-elf, which is what he is, and he is dressed for a hunt: high boots, dark coat, a sword at his hip that is too fine to be a working weapon, and an expression of open and genuine interest.

He is, by any measure that would apply in Haven, at least several hundred years old. He does not look it. He looks like a man in his early forties who has been keeping active.

*"You fight well,"* he says, in Common. *"Better than most. Most run. The ones who don't run, usually don't fight like that. What are you after, that you're crossing the mountains in late autumn with letters of royal authority in your bags?"*

He already knows. He is asking because he wants to hear how they describe it.

*Tellaris's full statistics appear in Appendix A.*

---

**After Defeat**

If the party defeats Tellaris and leaves him alive, he comes to rest sitting against a rock in the manner of someone who has just finished running a distance they are satisfied with. He is not distressed. He is not posturing. He has been fully defeated and he acknowledges it without apparent difficulty.

He will answer three questions. He treats this as a compact and he will not violate it; his answers will be accurate, specific, and no more helpful than the question requires. He will not answer questions about Haven's politics (outside his interest), but he will answer questions about the Wild Hunt, the First World, the mountain passes, what lies in Aldenmere, or the nature of the fey. He knows what the party is after. He considers the Sword Quest legitimate, in the way that only the hunted have standing to call any quest legitimate, and he will acknowledge this if asked.

He offers his longsword as the prize. He does not give it with warmth; he gives it as a man who has accepted the terms of a contest he entered. The sword is a fair weapon. It is not the sword he would have chosen to end his life with, but it is a fair weapon, and he gives it properly.

*The +2 keen longsword given by Tellaris should be treated as a standard prize item for a level 8 character; the GM may add one additional property if the party's performance was exceptional.*

**Level 8 Gate:** Awarded upon resolution of the Wild Hunt encounter — whether by surviving all three waves and Tellaris, returning from the First World, or any other means the party finds to break free of the Hunt's grasp.

---

### The Weather Closes

Within a week of the Wild Hunt encounter, the weather changes. The specific time of year — the autumnal equinox just passed — means that the first serious weather of autumn arrives without much warning: a storm that comes from the northwest and drops the temperature ten degrees in an afternoon, deposits two inches of snow on the upper passes, and makes clear that the question is no longer whether the party will have to winter before the crossing, but where.

The GM should make this a decision, not a fact: the party has approximately five days before the passes become genuinely dangerous, and within that window there are three options available to them.

---

### The Three Shelter Options

The party will learn of their options from the terrain itself (Survival DC 18 reveals signs of a dwarven settlement, smoke from fires in a specific direction; DC 20 reveals signs of a fey community), from Tellaris if they thought to ask him, from the Ghost of the Taken if that encounter occurred, or from the werebear if she was befriended in Chapter 2. The options are not equally visible and a party that makes no effort to find alternatives will likely stumble into the own-camp option by default.

**Option One — Keldur Hold (Dwarven Settlement)**

Keldur Hold is a small dwarven mining and survey community, established about sixty years ago by a team from the earldom of Kharas who followed a silver vein east across the mountain foothills. The vein played out after thirty years; the community stayed. They mine less now but they know the passes, they have stores, and they are accustomed to sheltering travelers — Haven's frontier is one of their trade partners.

*Atmosphere:* Practical, warm in the literal sense, no-nonsense. The Keldur dwarves have opinions about Haven's politics and no investment in telling them diplomatically. They have excellent beer and poor bread. The community's leader is an elder named Gritta, who is approximately one hundred and forty years old, has heard every kind of story from every kind of traveler, and will tell the party exactly what she thinks of the Sword Quest with the same tone she would use to appraise a piece of ore.

*Winter Activities:* Characters who winter at Keldur Hold may spend downtime points on the following activities, in addition to those listed in the Winter Downtime Framework:
- *Craft with dwarven tools* — a character with an appropriate Craft skill may produce one item per month at half normal cost, using the Hold's forge and workshop.
- *Mountain training* — the dwarves know the passes in a way that can be taught; a character who spends a full month in this activity gains a permanent +2 competence bonus on Survival checks made in mountain terrain.
- *Gritta's history* — Gritta has been in the foothills for sixty years. She knows the mountains from the inside, the fey from a respectful distance, and the Wild Hunt's habits from the kind of proximity that makes a person very careful about the autumnal equinox. A party that sits with her long enough — in the way that sitting with old people actually requires, which is patient and not on schedule — will receive a detailed briefing on the mountain crossing, hints about what lies on the far side, and, if they have treated her well, a piece of information she has not shared with any traveler before: the name of a dwarven trading partner in Aldenmere who may prove useful.

*Magic Economy:* Keldur Hold has one item of interest: a helm of the dwarven halls, a modest dwarven-crafted enchantment that grants Darkvision 60 ft. and a +2 competence bonus on Perception checks underground. The Hold will sell it for 5,000 gp or trade it for something of comparable value; it is not for offer simply because the party has arrived.

**Option Two — The Glade of the Turning (Fey Settlement)**

The fey community in the western mountain foothills is not a settlement in any sense the party would recognize. It is a collection of presences that occupy a set of old-growth stands and rocky outcroppings, maintaining something between cooperation and conversation, and wintering here means wintering in the way that the fey winter: with the same cold, the same dark, and the same strange sense that the season is being experienced deliberately rather than endured.

*Atmosphere:* Strange, beautiful, occasionally alarming. The fey here are not under Elandra's authority — her court is a week west — but they know of her, and a party that carries some evidence of favorable dealings with Elandra (a gift from her, a positive report that preceded them through fey communication channels) will be received differently than one that does not. Wintering here requires ongoing attention to fey etiquette; lapses are not punished harshly, but they are noted.

*Winter Activities:* Characters who winter at the Glade may spend downtime points on the following activities:
- *Fey instruction* — any character who wishes to improve their understanding of fey magic or the First World may spend a month working with an old sprite named Tessel, who has theories about everything and is almost always at least partially right. This grants a permanent +4 competence bonus on Knowledge (planes) checks relating to fey and the First World.
- *First World meditation* — a character with the ability to cast spells may spend a month in intensive magical meditation. At the end of the month, they may add one spell to their spell list (spellcasters who learn spells normally) or known spells (spontaneous casters); the spell must come from the fey tradition and must be chosen from the following list: *entangle, obscuring mist, charm person, deep slumber, tree stride, transport via plants, mislead, veil.* This ability functions once per character.
- *Revelation 2 deepening* — the fey of the Glade remember the displacement with the kind of specificity that three hundred years of oral tradition produces. A party that spends the winter here and engages seriously with this history will receive the full Revelation 2 before they reach Aldenmere, rather than upon arrival.

*Magic Economy:* The fey of the Glade deal in service and meaning rather than gold. They have three items in their collective care that they will part with for appropriate consideration — not money, but acts of genuine value:
- *Cloak of the wild shape* (functions as a *cloak of resistance* +2 with the additional property of granting the *wild shape* ability for 1 hour/day, as a druid of the character's level; if the character is a druid, add two druid levels to effective druid level for this ability): price is a piece of genuine music, never heard before, composed and performed in the Glade.
- *Boots of the woodland stride* (as *boots of elvenkind* with the additional benefit of *woodland stride* as the druid ability): price is truthful answers to seven questions, none of which can be refused.
- *Wand of call lightning* (25 charges): price is three months of honest labor for the Glade — chopping no trees, burning nothing, and following fey direction without question.

**Option Three — Own Camp**

A party that does not seek alternatives will find a workable site on its own: a south-facing rock shelf with a natural windbreak, a forest depression that keeps the worst of the wind out, adequate fuel nearby. It is not comfortable. It is survivable.

*Atmosphere:* Quiet. The mountains in winter are profoundly quiet in a way that city-trained or even frontier-trained characters may not have experienced before. The party will spend a great deal of time together, in close quarters, with nothing external to direct attention toward. This is, depending on the party, either a source of tension or of depth.

*Winter Activities:* Characters who winter in their own camp may spend downtime points on the following activities:
- *Watch rotation* — setting and maintaining a disciplined watch schedule provides a practical benefit for the spring crossing: the party cannot be surprised for the first three days of mountain travel.
- *Training* — any character may spend a month in focused physical or martial training. At the end of the month, they gain a +1 competence bonus on attack rolls or saving throws (one choice, specified at the start of training) that lasts until the end of Act III. This can only be chosen once per character.
- *Journal work* — a character who keeps a detailed journal of the winter (Intelligence check DC 14 over the course of the winter) produces a document that, if shared with Harold II or another appropriate figure on the party's return, constitutes a genuine piece of historical record. This has no mechanical effect but is treated by those who receive it as evidence of the party's character.

*Magic Economy:* None beyond what the party carries - the charcters may perform their own crafting if they have the supplies to do so, if they can forage them, or trade for them with one of the nearby settlements. If they brought enough supplies, they will be fine. If they did not, they will need to forage — Survival DC 14 for the party's rations, DC 18 in the worst of winter.

---

### Winter Downtime

The Winter Downtime Framework in the Running the Adventure section describes the full mechanical structure: three months of downtime organized around two-week periods, available downtime points, and the general categories of activity. The activities listed above for each shelter option are additions to those standard activities, available only at the specified location.

*Duration:* The party winters from approximately the second week of October through the last week of March — roughly five and a half months. In game terms, this is ten downtime periods of two weeks each.

*The pass reopens:* In late March, the pass becomes navigable again. A Survival DC 16 check can identify the specific week the weather will be stable enough to attempt the crossing. The dwarves of Keldur Hold know this without a check.

**Conditional: The Return of the Dark Knight**

*This section applies only if the party defeated Sir Aemond dishonorably during Quest 5 in Act I — that is, if they used outside assistance, external magic, or any other means that violated the terms of an honorable single combat.*

The curse that bound Sir Aemond does not break on a dishonorable defeat. He regenerates. He remembers the violation — not because he has recovered his identity (he has not; the seal on his memory holds) but because the curse itself carries the memory of the broken condition, in the way that a wound remembers the blade that made it. When the curse regenerates him, it orients him toward the party. It takes him several months to find them.

He arrives in the second month of winter.

The GM should give the party signs before he arrives: a traveler who passed through three days ago who described a man in sealed black armor asking directions, moving east from Haven; a sense among the dwarves of Keldur Hold or the fey of the Glade that something wrong-shaped is in the foothills; the particular quality of silence that precedes a challenge from something that cannot be reasoned with.

When Sir Aemond arrives, he does not attack. He stops at the edge of the party's shelter and waits. If the party does not come out, he will wait until they do. He will wait a very long time.

The challenge he offers is the same challenge he has offered to every knight he has encountered: single combat, one opponent, honorable terms. His spoken challenge is the same formula it always is — the curse has preserved it like everything else. The party will recognize it.

The terms are the same as before. If the fight is won honorably — no outside assistance, no external magic during the duel — the curse breaks on this defeat as it would have broken before. Sir Aemond recovers. He will be confused, disoriented, and entirely without context for the five or six months that have passed since his last clear memory, but he will be himself, and the curse will be finished.

If the fight is won dishonorably again, he regenerates again. He will return in a year.

*A party that has traveled to Aldenmere and back before he catches them again will face him in Act IV instead, which the Act IV Crisis Framework accounts for. A party that defeats him honorably in winter quarters removes one Act IV crisis entirely and gains Sir Aemond as a complex figure whose recovery arc begins here rather than on return.*

*Note on Sir Aemond's capabilities in winter quarters:* He has not changed from his Act I encounter statistics. The party is now level 8, with a full winter's worth of preparation and (potentially) significant magic items from Act II. The combat is meaningfully easier than it would have been in Act I if fought honorably at the time. This is intentional. The difficulty of the dishonor path is not the second fight — it is the cost of the delay, the disruption to winter, and the loss of the straightforward Act IV outcome that honorable defeat would have provided.

---

### The Pass Reopens

The crossing takes approximately ten days in good weather. It is not a comfortable ten days. The altitude, the cold (still present even in late March at pass elevation), and the sheer scale of the mountains are genuinely affecting: even a party that has been in the foothills all winter will find the passes themselves a different category of experience.

There is nothing in the passes that is designed to stop them. There are weather events, altitude sickness (Fort DC 15, failure means half speed and –2 on attack rolls and skill checks for the day), and the ordinary dangers of high-altitude travel. The GM may use these to add texture and cost without introducing combat encounters in a section that has already had more than its share. The crossing should feel like the end of something and the beginning of something, which is what it is.

On the final day of the descent, the mountains open onto the eastern foothills, and the party sees Aldenmere for the first time.

*The GM should describe this with care. It is the first genuinely new country Haven's people have seen in three hundred years. The party has earned the moment.*

*Chapter 4 begins on the eastern descent.*

---

# ACT III: THE FAR EAST

## Chapter 4: The Far Crossing
*(Levels 8–9)*

### Chapter Overview

Chapter 4 is the bridge between the journey and the destination. The mountains are behind the party. Haven is further away than it has ever been. What lies ahead is a country no one from Haven has seen in three centuries — and the first thing the party encounters in it is a lord and his manor that are not entirely what they appear to be.

The Lord Wustan encounter is a test of a specific virtue: restraint in the presence of abundance. The party will be offered hospitality and told that everything within the manor is at their disposal. The test is not whether they believe this. The test is whether they take it literally.

This chapter requires the GM to play Lord Wustan and Lady Tamera with patience and care. Neither of them will explain the rules. Neither of them will confirm the nature of the test. Lady Tamera will, at the appointed time, tell a story. Whether the party connects the story to their situation is entirely their own doing.

**Chapter 4 Milestone Leveling:**
- *Level 9* — awarded upon departing Lord Wustan's manor under any favorable outcome (the party has satisfied the conditions of the manor, voluntarily or otherwise)

---

### The Descent

The eastern descent from the mountains takes three days. It is, in most respects, easier than the western approach — the slope is gentler, the path more established, and the altitude drops quickly. The cold remains, but the air changes on the second day: it is softer, carrying a quality of moisture that the western foothills did not have, the smell of old forest and wet stone.

On the morning of the third day, the mountains open.

> *You have been descending through scrub and scattered pine for two days, and then the path turns south and drops steeply, and you come around a rock buttress and find that the mountains simply end — not gradually but at a specific point, where the slope levels and the trees begin. The trees are different from the Wild's trees. They are older, and taller, and they grow further apart, each one occupying a space that its size has earned over centuries, the canopy above incomplete enough to allow long shafts of morning light to reach the ground. The ground itself is covered in something the Wild does not have: moss, undisturbed moss, thick and vivid, covering every surface that is not in direct sunlight. There is no path. There is open forest, extending further east than you can see, without a sound except the wind and the occasional distant call of a bird you cannot identify.*
>
> *Somewhere beneath that canopy is the Shrine.*

---

### The Road into Aldenmere

Aldenmere is not Haven. The differences are apparent within the first hour of travel and accumulate throughout the day. There are no roads of the kind Haven builds — no cut stone, no maintained ditches, no milestones. There are paths: deer paths widened by human use, forest tracks marked by subtle signs (notches in bark, stacked stones at intervals) that suggest regular travel without suggesting infrastructure. The settlements, if any exist within a day's travel of the descent, do not announce themselves.

The party will not meet anyone on their first day in Aldenmere. The forest is inhabited — they will see signs of habitation, smell woodsmoke, find a path that is too well-worn for game alone — but nobody appears. They are being observed and assessed before being approached. This is not hostility; it is caution, the kind that three hundred years of managing the Pentad's western neighbor has made habitual.

On the evening of the second day, the mist comes in.

It is not a usual fog — it moves with a purposefulness that weather does not have, filling the low ground between the trees from the east, thickening as the light fails, until by the time the party considers making camp, visibility has dropped to twenty feet and is still dropping. Survival checks to navigate (DC 18) reveal only that the path they have been following has become indistinguishable from the surrounding forest floor. The mist carries the sound of barking — hunting hounds, distant, moving parallel to the party's position rather than toward it.

Then, through the mist, a figure on horseback.

---

### Lord Wustan

> *He sits very still on a grey horse whose coat is near the same color as the mist. Around him, barely visible, you count perhaps a dozen hounds — large dogs, pale-coated, utterly silent. They are not tracking. They are waiting, in the particular way that well-trained dogs wait: without impatience, without distraction, with the absolute concentration of things that have been taught to attend. The rider's gear is fine — better than fine, the kind of quality that stops you and makes you look again. His sword is thin and elegant, built for a different school of fighting than Haven's. He carries a crossbow at his saddle, a weapon so unusual you may have to name it to each other before you recognize what it is. His expression, when he sees you, is not surprised.*
>
> *"Good evening," he says. His language has an accent you have not heard before. "You are a long way from Haven."*

Lord Wustan is a tall man, composed without being cold, quiet without being withdrawn. He speaks without hurry. He introduces himself as a noble of these lands, offers no surname or title beyond the name, and expresses no surprise that the party is from Haven — he says only that it has been some years since anyone came over the passes, that the mist is not good traveling weather, and that his house is close and has more rooms than it needs.

He does not press. He offers once and leaves the rest to the party. If they have any interest in shelter, the hounds will fall into formation around them, and he will lead them east along a path that was not there before.

*Lord Wustan will answer direct questions about himself with deflection that is graceful — not lies, but answers that are technically accurate and functionally empty. He knows who the party is and roughly why they are here. He will not reveal this knowledge until the evening in the study. He is not trying to deceive them; he is being careful, in the way of a man who has learned that too much too early rarely helps.*

---

### The Manor

The path through the mist takes approximately twenty minutes. No one asks how a manor can be twenty minutes away from a path in open forest that showed no signs of habitation. The answer is available, but not yet.

> *The house resolves out of the mist gradually — first a shape, then an outline, then detail. It is large, larger than you expected from a lord of a wilderness territory. Several small turrets rise from the roofline, their stonework precise and without weathering, as if the house has never suffered a hard winter. The gables are broad, built from timber of a deep brown that has never been painted, the grain of it visible from here, elaborate and fine. Every surface you can see is the product of sustained craftsmanship: carved lintels, dressed stone that fits without visible mortar, shutters hung straight in their frames. Whatever this lord is a lord of, it has not been a hardship.*
>
> *The door opens before he knocks.*

The interior is everything the exterior promises and then more. The floors are parquet — a technique Haven's craftsmen could achieve but rarely do at this scale — the wood laid in patterns that shift in the candlelight as you move through the rooms. The walls carry tapestries of a kind that would be notable in any of Haven's great halls: detailed, vivid, depicting scenes from a history the party will not immediately recognize. Sculptures stand in alcoves and at corridor junctions. Paintings hang in the larger rooms. There is no object in the house that is not beautiful and no room that is not full of things worth looking at. The scale is not excessive — it is not a palace — but the density of quality is extraordinary, and the party will feel it the moment they cross the threshold: this is not a house that has accumulated these things; it is a house that has always had them.

The hounds do not enter. They settle outside the door.

---

### Lady Tamera

Lord Wustan brings them to a sitting room where his wife is waiting.

Lady Tamera is a woman of extraordinary beauty — not in the conspicuous, theatrical way of a court favorite constructed for display, but in a deeper, more complete way, the kind of beauty that belongs to old paintings of subjects who never knew they were beautiful. She is dressed in dark velvet and a great deal of fine jewelry, none of it ostentatious, all of it chosen rather than accumulated. She rises when they enter and greets them by name — if they gave their names to the lord, she already knows them; if they did not, she uses them anyway, softly, without comment.

She insists they join the couple for dinner. Before dinner, she leads each party member personally to a private bedroom, where a bath has been drawn and clothes of excellent quality — fitted to the character, in the character's style, in Haven fashion, in a house that has not seen a visitor from Haven in years — are laid out. She does not explain how. She does not appear to notice that it requires explanation.

Dinner is excellent. She serves it herself. There are no servants — this will become apparent during the stay; the food simply appears, the rooms are cleaned without anyone doing it, the fires maintain themselves. For now, the quality of the dinner is sufficient to defer the question.

At dinner she is warm, attentive, and present in the way that good hosts are present: noticing what the party needs before they ask, steering the conversation toward ease, offering and never demanding. She will ask each party member one question of genuine personal interest — not probing, just curious — and listen carefully to the answer.

---

### The Evening in the Study

After dinner, Lord Wustan invites the party into his study.

The study is the most personal room in the house: books, a writing desk with papers that are not for inspection, a fire, and a sense of use that the more formal rooms do not have. He settles comfortably, pours something warm, and talks.

He talks the way people talk who are interested in other people: asking questions, following threads, going where the conversation naturally leads. If the party is forthcoming about the nature of their quest, he will eventually reach it — and when he does, his response is genuine delight.

*"A Sword Quest. I haven't heard that phrase in — it's been a very long time."*

He tells them about Haven's founding, from his perspective — the perspective of someone who has watched Haven from the outside for centuries, who knew Cuthbert's descendants by reputation and occasionally in person, who has met two previous parties on similar quests (neither name will be familiar; they did not succeed). He does not say why they failed. He tells the stories with a kind of careful affection, the way people speak of the dead.

Then he says he must attend to urgent business, and will be gone for a few days, and offers them the hospitality of the house while they wait.

*"I will escort you personally to the Shrine when I return. The mists are thick this time of year and the path is not obvious to newcomers. It would be very easy to lose your way, and I do not want that for you."*

He recommends they wait. He tells them that whatever is within his house is at their disposal while they stay. He says this once, plainly, and does not repeat it.

*A Sense Motive check (DC 22) reveals that Lord Wustan is not calm. Beneath the composed surface, he is agitated — the particular agitation of a man who is hoping something will go the right way and is not certain it will. He does not reveal what he is hoping for. He does not ask for anything. He says goodnight and leaves early the next morning before the party wakes.*

---

### The Days of Waiting

The manor operates by rules the party must discover for themselves. None of these rules will be stated directly. Lady Tamera will not explain them. The house will simply respond to what the party does.

**Lady Tamera's Presence**

When the lord is away, the lady is nearly absent. The party will catch glimpses of her — a figure at the end of a corridor, a shadow at the edge of a room — but she will have disappeared before they reach where she was. The only times she is reliably present are the evening meals, which appear in the dining room at the same hour each day regardless of anything else in the house.

At dinner she is as warm and attentive as before. She will ask each evening whether everything is to their liking. If the party has not taken anything from the house, she will gently note — without emphasis, as a matter of practical information — that the lord said whatever they desire within the house is at their disposal.

A party member who attempts to press Lady Tamera on personal questions — about herself, the lord, the house, its history, the story behind any of its contents — must succeed at a Will save (DC 18) to pursue the subject beyond the surface. She is not using magic; she simply has the quality of a very still pool, and looking at it, most people find they have stopped asking what is beneath. Characters who succeed at the Will save may continue the conversation, and may ask one direct question to which she will give an honest but minimal answer. If pressed further, she will excuse herself with perfect grace and disappear. She will not reappear until the next meal.

**The House's Impossible Layout**

Any attempt to map the manor fails. A party that makes a systematic effort to understand the floor plan — walking every corridor, marking every door — will find that the map they produce does not match the house they are in. Rooms appear that were not there before; corridors lead to different places on different attempts; stairways connect floors in ways that are not geometrically consistent with the exterior dimensions. Magical detection of the layout (Detect Magic, Arcane Sight, or similar) reveals a strong aura of transmutation and conjuration permeating every room, but reveals nothing about the layout itself.

The party will always find their bedrooms around nightfall. They will always find the dining room at meal times. They will find other rooms when they are not looking for the dining room or their bedrooms.

Some of the rooms they find are extraordinary. A long gallery of portraits whose subjects shift when not observed directly. A library in a language that is not theirs and is not Elven and has a script that looks designed for hands with more fingers than human hands have. A conservatory containing plants that cannot be alive this far north in winter, but are. A small locked room that is not locked when approached for the second time.

*The GM should treat exploration of the manor as an opportunity for the party to encounter interesting things rather than a puzzle to solve. The layout is not a threat; it is a character.*

**Attempting to Leave**

If the party attempts to leave the manor before Lord Wustan returns, they are not stopped. The doors open. The hounds outside do not prevent their departure. The mist simply receives them.

Within twenty minutes, the mist has closed in completely. Navigation checks at any DC fail — not because the characters roll poorly, but because the mist is not a natural obstacle and does not respond to skill. Magical navigation (Find the Path, Know Direction) will point toward the correct direction but the correct direction leads through terrain that loops. After an hour, the characters will hear the hounds barking, closer now. After another twenty minutes, the manor will appear ahead of them.

Each day the party attempts to leave in this fashion, Lord Wustan is delayed by one additional day.

Lady Tamera, if asked directly about leaving, will say: *"The only path open to you until my lord returns leads west. I am sorry I cannot say more."* She means this literally. The path west — back through the mountains — is accessible. The path east is not, until the manor releases them.

**The Item Rule**

Lord Wustan told the party that whatever is within his house is at their disposal. This is true. The party may take anything in the manor. The house is full of extraordinary things: fine weapons, clothing of exceptional quality, books, art objects, coins in several currencies. All of it is genuinely available.

The rule, which no one will state:

*A party member who takes one item from the house incurs no penalty.*

*A party member who takes more than one item causes a delay. Lord Wustan will not return until the excess items are returned to the house. The items do not have to be returned to specific locations — placing them anywhere inside the manor suffices. Lady Tamera will not confirm this mechanism, but the timing will be suggestive to an attentive party.*

*A party member who takes nothing from the house earns the manor's favor.*

The party must work this out for themselves. Lady Tamera's regular reminder about the lord's offer is a clue, not a nudge toward generosity — she is pointing at the subject, not at the answer. A character with Knowledge (arcana) or Knowledge (local) may attempt a DC 20 check; success suggests that fey-bonded places often have conditions on the hospitality they offer, though the specific terms here are unusual enough that the check provides direction rather than certainty.

---

### The Elm Tree Room

When the conditions of the manor's test have been met — when no party member is carrying more than one item from the house, regardless of whether this was achieved accidentally or deliberately, and at least three days have passed without the party trying to press east through the mist — the house changes.

On the day before Lord Wustan returns, the party will find themselves in a room they have not found before.

> *The room is wide and old — the ceiling higher than the floors above it should permit, the walls paneled in dark wood that has not been shaped so much as grown into these dimensions, the grain of it visible and intricate in the candlelight. At the center of the room stands a tree.*
>
> *It is an elm, or it was: ancient, gnarled at the base, its trunk wider than two people standing with arms outstretched, its branches rising and dividing and dividing again until they meet the rafters and continue into them, not through gaps but through the wood itself, as if the tree and the house share the same structure in this room, as if neither could stand without the other. The bark of the trunk is completely smooth at head height. Someone has touched it, thousands of times, always in the same place.*

Lady Tamera will be present in this room. She will be standing near the tree but not touching it. When the party enters, she will look at them for a moment, and then she will tell them a story. She will tell it in the way of someone who has told it many times and has learned to tell it without becoming undone by the telling.

---

**Lady Tamera's Story**

*"Long ago, a mortal man found a dryad by a pool of still water and fell in love with her. She rebuffed him - This was sensible of her - and he went away.*

*"But he came back the next evening with a gift and a poem and a word of genuine feeling, and he did this again the evening after that, and the evening after that. He did it for a year and a day — every evening, without exception, without growing impatient, without asking for anything in return. At the end of that year and a day, the dryad agreed to marry him, on one condition: that he would never ask her about her true nature.*

*"He agreed. They were married. He had to leave after a short time, as men do, to attend to the things that men attend to, and he promised her he would return in another year and a day with a house built for her — a house fit for her, he said, which she understood to mean a house that could contain her entirely.*

*"He was as good as his word on this point as on all others. He found the greatest tree in the forest and felled it, and used every inch of its wood to build a house the likes of which had never been built by a single pair of hands. He was proud of it. He went then to fetch his wife and could not find her. He searched the length and breadth of the land for a year and a day, and found nothing. He went to the sorceress of the coast and begged her to tell him where his wife had gone.*

*"The sorceress told him. The tree he had felled was his wife's tree. She had died when he cut it down. He had not known. This is often the case with harm: the cause and the effect are separated by time and distance and a complete absence of malice, and none of that makes it less harm.*

*"He asked the sorceress to restore her.*

*"The sorceress said she could. The dryad's spirit had passed into the wood of the house, and it lived there still, and could be called back into something like life — but she would never be able to leave the bounds of the house again. And the price was this: the man would agree to be bound to the house as well. He would remain there forever, he and his wife together, as guardians of the pass that divided the old world from the new, turning back those who were unworthy and guiding forward those who were not.*

*"He agreed without taking time to think about it. In my experience, some agreements are like that."*

She finishes the story. She does not say anything else on the subject. She does not confirm whether she was the dryad or whether the lord was the man in the story. She will not answer questions about it — not because she is concealing the truth, but because she believes the party is capable of understanding what she has told them and she does not see the value in stating it plainly when it has already been said.

She does say one more thing, before she moves toward the door:

*"Whatever you take from this house when you leave — take it with the thought that it was freely given. There is a difference between accepting a gift and taking what is available. I hope you have learned to tell them apart."*

She leaves. The door they came in through is open.

---

### Lord Wustan Returns

Lord Wustan arrives on the following morning, in better spirits than when he left. He does not explain where he went or what the urgent business was. He greets the party as if they have been staying in his house for a pleasant visit rather than an uncanny one, and he takes stock of them — not accusingly, just carefully, the way a man takes stock of things that matter to him.

**If the party took nothing from the house:**

Both Lord Wustan and Lady Tamera are present at breakfast. The morning has a quality that the previous days did not — something in the light, in the ease of the rooms, in the way the hounds outside behave — that suggests the house itself is in a different mood. Lord Wustan thanks the party for their conduct. He does not use the word "test." He and his wife, together, present each party member with a gift: a magic item chosen for the character specifically, useful for the journey ahead.
